  "output_summary": "All theorems in `problems/problem-2.lean` have been proved. The proof establishes that the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse provides the minimum Euclidean norm solution to the linear system Ax = b.\n\nThree helper lemmas were introduced and proved:\n1. **`xa_idempotent`**: Shows that XA is idempotent ((XA)² = XA), derived from XAX = X.\n2. **`pseudoinverse_feasible`**: Shows that X·b is feasible (A·(X·b) = b), using AXA = A and the existence of a feasible point.\n3. **`symmetric_idempotent_norm_le`**: Shows that for any symmetric idempotent matrix P, ‖Py‖² ≤ ‖y‖² — the key optimality inequality using the Pythagorean decomposition y = Py + (I-P)y.\n\nThe main theorem combines these: feasibility follows from lemma 2, and optimality follows by noting that X·b = (XA)·y for any feasible y, then applying lemma 3 with P = XA (which is symmetric idempotent by the Moore-Penrose conditions).\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no remaining `sorry` statements.",

{

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/problem-3.lean. Keep all theorem statements and existing definitions unchanged. Use Lean 4.28/mathlib 4.28. Return a compiling Lean project.",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `primal_dual_linear_program_optimalValue_le_convexLogisticProgram_optimalValue_le` in `problems/problem-3.lean`.\n\nThe theorem shows that for a convex logistic program with optimal value q* and a corresponding primal-dual linear program pair with optimal value p*, under the assumptions that the primal LP is feasible, strong duality holds (p* is finite), and there exists a dual optimal solution z* with z* ≤ 1 componentwise:\n\n  p* ≤ q* ≤ p* + m·log(2)/μ\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **Analytical bounds on log(1+exp(t))**: Three lemmas establishing log(1+exp(t)) ≥ 0, log(1+exp(t)) ≥ t, and log(1+exp(t)) ≤ log(2) when t ≤ 0.\n\n2. **Scaled versions**: For μ > 0, (1/μ)·log(1+exp(μs)) ≥ max(0,s) and (1/μ)·log(1+exp(μs)) ≤ log(2)/μ when s ≤ 0.\n\n3. **Structural lemmas**: \n   - The logistic objective dominates the linear objective plus a max-penalty term\n   - The linear-plus-penalty term dominates the dual objective (using 0 ≤ z* ≤ 1)\n   - For primal-feasible points, the logistic objective is bounded by the linear objective plus m·log(2)/μ\n\n4. **Main theorem**: Combines the helper lemmas with EReal infimum arguments.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",
  "has_input_files": true,
  "has_output_files": true,
  "input_prompt": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/problem-3.lean. Keep all theorem statements and existing definitions unchanged. Use Lean 4.28/mathlib 4.28. Return a compiling Lean project.",

This is done by proving that t ↦ -t log t is concave on [0, ∞) (via second derivative test: d²/dt²(-t log t) = -1/t ≤ 0), then using that a sum of concave functions is concave.\n\n2. **Convexity of the feasible set**: The feasible set C (nonneg, sum-to-one, moment constraints) is shown to be convex as an intersection of convex sets (the nonneg orthant and affine hyperplanes).\n\nThe file compiles without errors or `sorry` statements.   "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/problem-5.lean. Keep all theorem statements and existing definitions unchanged. Use Lean 4.28/mathlib 4.28. Return a compiling Lean project.",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `exp_integral_on_nonnegative_orthant_finite_iff` in `problems/problem-5.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states that for θ ∈ ℝⁿ, the integral of exp(θᵀx) over the nonnegative orthant ℝ₊ⁿ is finite if and only if all components θᵢ < 0, and in that case the integral equals ∏(-θᵢ)⁻¹ with normalizer ∏(-θᵢ).\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`indicator_orthant_eq_prod`**: The indicator of the orthant times the exponential kernel factors as a product of 1D indicator functions.\n\n2. **`exp_mul_integrableOn_Ici_iff`**: For a single variable, exp(c·x) is integrable on [0,∞) if and only if c < 0.\n\n3. **`integral_exp_mul_Ici`**: For c < 0, the integral ∫₀^∞ exp(c·x) dx = (-c)⁻¹.\n\n4. **`integrableOn_orthant_of_neg`**: When all θᵢ < 0, the product kernel is integrable on the orthant (using Fubini's theorem for finite products).\n\n5. **`neg_of_integrableOn_orthant`**: If the kernel is integrable on the orthant, then all θᵢ < 0 (by showing that if some θⱼ ≥ 0, the function is bounded below by a positive constant on a subset of infinite measure).\n\n6. **`integral_orthant_eq_prod_inv`**: The integral over the orthant equals ∏(-θᵢ)⁻¹ (via indicator factoring and Fubini).\n\nThe main theorem follows by combining these pieces. The file compiles with no `sorry` and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

This function is C², has zero gradient and zero (hence PSD) Hessian at 0, but is not a local minimum since x³ takes negative values in every neighborhood of 0.\n\n2. **`strict_local_minimizer_without_positive_definite_hessian`**: Uses f(x) = x⁴ with x* = 0. This function is C², has a strict local minimum at 0 (since x⁴ > 0 for x ≠ 0), has zero gradient at 0, but the Hessian at 0 is zero (not positive definite), witnessed by v = 1 giving ∇²f(0)(1,1) = 0.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry`, warnings, or errors. {

  "created_at": "2026-04-29T16:43:59.165512",

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/problem-7.lean. Keep all theorem statements and existing definitions unchanged. Use Lean 4.28/mathlib 4.28. Return a compiling Lean project.",
  "output_summary": "I made substantial progress on the two theorems in `problems/problem-7.lean`, but was unable to fully eliminate all `sorry`s due to the deep mathematical infrastructure required.\n\n## What was accomplished\n\nThe file now compiles without errors (with `sorry` warnings). I decomposed the two main theorems into a structured proof using 10+ helper lemmas, most of which are fully proved:\n\n### Theorem 1: `inf_prod_quadratic_forms_eq_prod_smallest_eigenvalues`\nThis theorem states that the product of the k smallest eigenvalues equals the infimum of products of quadratic forms over orthonormal matrices. The proof is structured as:\n- **`prod_quadratic_set_nonempty`** ✅ — The infimum set is nonempty\n- **`prod_quadratic_set_bddBelow`** ✅ — The infimum set is bounded below  \n- **`eigenvectors_achieve_product`** ✅ — Eigenvectors achieve the product (infimum is attained)\n- **`quadratic_product_ge_eigenvalue_product`** ✅ (modulo one sub-lemma) — Lower bound on products\n  - **`hadamard_ineq`** ✅ — Hadamard's inequality: det(A) ≤ ∏ A_ii for positive definite A\n  - **`pos_def_congruence`** ✅ — V^T X V is positive definite when X is and V^T V = I\n  - **`congruence_diagonal`** ✅ — Diagonal entries of V^T X V equal the quadratic forms\n  - **`det_congruence_ge_prod_eigenvalues`** ❌ (sorry) — Fischer's inequality: det(V^T X V) ≥ product of k smallest eigenvalues\n\n### Theorem 2: `sum_log_smallest_eigenvalues_concave`\n❌ (sorry) — This theorem about concavity of the sum of logs of smallest eigenvalues remains unproved.\n\n## Remaining `sorry`s\n\nTwo `sorry`s remain:\n\n1. **Fischer's inequality** (`det_congruence_ge_prod_eigenvalues`, line 164): This requires the Cauchy-Binet formula (`det(A^T B) = Σ_S det(A_S) det(B_S)`), which is not available in Mathlib. The proof would show that `det(V^T X V)` is a convex combination of products of eigenvalues over k-subsets, and the minimum such product is the product of the k smallest eigenvalues.\n\n2. **Concavity theorem** (`sum_log_smallest_eigenvalues_concave`, line 270): This depends on Theorem 1 and additionally requires showing that the infimum of concave functions is concave, applied to log-transformed quadratic forms.\n\n## Key helper lemmas proved\n- `pos_def_quadratic_pos` — Positive definiteness implies positive quadratic forms\n- `eigenvector_quadform_eq_eigenvalue` — Quadratic form at eigenvector equals eigenvalue\n- `eigenvector_submatrix_ortho` — Eigenvector submatrices are orthogonal\n- `hadamard_ineq` — Hadamard's inequality for positive definite matrices (non-trivial!)\n\nNote: `set_option maxHeartbeats 800000` was added at the top of the file to accommodate the computationally intensive Hadamard inequality proof.",

  "has_output_files": true,

This theorem establishes the KKT optimality conditions for the log-determinant semidefinite program: minimize tr(CX) − log det X subject to diag(X) = 1.\n\nThe theorem states that X is optimal if and only if:\n- X is symmetric and positive definite\n- X⁻¹ − C is diagonal\n- diag(X) = 1\n\nThe proof was decomposed into the following helper lemmas:\n\n**Basic helpers:**\n- `trace_diagonal_mul`, `trace_diagonal_eq_of_diag_eq` — trace properties for diagonal matrices\n- `Matrix.IsSymm.inv'` — inverse of symmetric matrix is symmetric\n- `det_inv_mul` — det(X⁻¹Y) = det(Y)/det(X)\n\n**Key inequality:**\n- `trace_sub_log_det_ge_card` — tr(A) − log det(A) ≥ n for PD matrix A (via spectral decomposition and AM-GM: λ − log λ ≥ 1)\n- `posDef_congruence` — congruence PAP^T preserves positive definiteness\n- `trace_inv_mul_sub_logdet_ge` — tr(X⁻¹Y) − log det(X⁻¹Y) ≥ n for PD X,Y (via matrix square root and congruence)\n\n**Calculus infrastructure:**\n- `differentiable_det_add_smul` — det(X + tΔ) is differentiable in t\n- `deriv_det_at_zero` — derivative of det(X + tΔ) at t=0 is det(X)·tr(X⁻¹Δ)\n- `deriv_logdet_at_zero` — derivative of log det(X + tΔ) at t=0 is tr(X⁻¹Δ)\n- `posDef_add_smul_eventually` — PD is open: X + tΔ is PD for small t\n\n**Forward direction** (optimality → KKT): Uses first-order optimality (derivative = 0 along feasible perturbations) to show X⁻¹ − C has zero off-diagonal entries.\n\n**Backward direction** (KKT → optimality): Uses the trace-log-det inequality and algebraic manipulation to show objective(Y) ≥ objective(X) for all feasible Y.\n\nThe proof compiles cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `dualCone_Kpol_eq_Khan` in `problems/problem-14.lean`, which states that the dual of the nonnegative polynomial cone equals the Hankel positive semidefinite cone.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into two directions:\n\n1. **⊆ direction** (`dualCone_Kpol_sub_Khan`): If z is in the dual cone of Kpol, then its Hankel matrix is PSD. The key idea is that for any vector v, the squared polynomial p(t)² = (∑ vᵢtⁱ)² is nonneg for all t, so its coefficient vector is in Kpol. The quadratic form v^T H v equals the inner product ⟪z, coeffs(p²)⟫, which is nonneg by dual cone membership.\n\n2. **⊇ direction** (`Khan_sub_dualCone_Kpol`): If the Hankel matrix is PSD, then z is in the dual cone. This required proving the classical **SOS (sum of squares) theorem for univariate polynomials**: every nonneg univariate real polynomial can be written as a sum of two squares. The proof uses strong induction on degree, factoring out real roots (which must have even multiplicity) and complex conjugate root pairs, using the Brahmagupta-Fibonacci identity for products of sums of two squares.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` and depends only on standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`). A `set_option maxHeartbeats 1600000` was added for the more complex ⊇ direction proof.",

  "output_summary": "Proved `psdCone_isSelfDual` in `problems/problem-18.lean`. The theorem shows the positive semidefinite cone is self-dual under the trace inner product.\n\nThe proof works in two directions:\n- **(⊆)** If Y is in the dual cone (tr(YX) ≥ 0 for all PSD X), then Y is PSD. This is shown by contradiction: if v·(Yv) < 0 for some v, then the rank-1 PSD matrix X = vvᵀ gives tr(YX) = v·(Yv) < 0, a contradiction.\n- **(⊇)** If Y is PSD, then tr(YX) ≥ 0 for all PSD X. Factor X = VVᵀ (since X is PSD), then tr(YX) = tr(VᵀYV), and VᵀYV is PSD (since Y is), so its trace (sum of diagonal entries, each ≥ 0 by PSD-ness) is non-negative.\n\nThe file compiles with no `sorry` and no errors.",

The main theorem `neg_gradient_mem_dualCone_of_concave_log_homogeneous` shows that for a proper cone K, if ψ is differentiable and concave on -int(K) with the log-homogeneity property ψ(sx) = ψ(x) + log(s), and y ≺_K 0, then -∇ψ(y) ∈ K* (the dual cone).\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`fderiv_apply_eq_sum`**: The sum ∑ᵢ (fderiv ℝ ψ y)(eᵢ) * xᵢ equals (fderiv ℝ ψ y)(x), by linearity.\n2. **`interior_smul_mem`**: Interior of a proper cone is closed under positive scaling.\n3. **`interior_add_cone_mem`**: int(K) + K ⊆ int(K) for a proper cone K.\n4. **`neg_interior_sub_smul_mem`**: y ∈ -int(K) and x ∈ K implies y - t·x ∈ -int(K) for t ≥ 0.\n5. **`neg_interior_smul_sub_mem`**: s·(y - t·x) ∈ -int(K) for s, t > 0.\n6. **`concave_first_order`**: Multivariate first-order concavity condition ψ(z) ≤ ψ(y) + Dψ(y)(z-y), derived from the 1D version.\n7. **`fderiv_self_eq_one`**: (fderiv ℝ ψ y)(y) = 1, derived from the log-homogeneity by differentiating ψ(sy) = ψ(y) + log(s) at s = 1.\n\nThe main proof applies the first-order concavity condition to z = s·(y - t·x) and combines with log-homogeneity to get: ψ(y-tx) + log(s) ≤ ψ(y) + s(1 - tD) - 1 where D = Dψ(y)(x). If D > 0, taking t = 2/D gives the RHS = ψ(y) - s - 1 → -∞ while LHS → +∞, yielding a contradiction.\n\nThe proof compiles cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "Both theorems in `problems/problem-36.lean` have been proved:\n\n1. **`inequality_constrained_problem_eq_unconstrained_supremum_reformulation`** — Shows that the optimal value of the inequality-constrained problem equals the optimal value of its unconstrained supremum reformulation.\n\n2. **`inequality_constrained_problem_and_unconstrained_supremum_reformulation_have_same_optimal_solutions`** — Shows that the set of optimal solutions coincides between the two formulations.\n\nThe proof strategy decomposes the problem using several helper lemmas:\n- For feasible points x, the supremum over λ ≥ 0 of the Lagrangian equals f(x) (since all constraint terms λᵢcᵢ(x) ≤ 0, the max is achieved at λ = 0).\n- For infeasible points x, some constraint cⱼ(x) > 0, so taking λⱼ → ∞ drives the Lagrangian to ⊤.\n- This means the unconstrained reformulation penalizes infeasibility with ⊤, making the two problems equivalent.\n\nThe project builds successfully with no `sorry` statements and only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`).",

The theorem states that if Y is a positive definite real symmetric matrix and Z - Y is positive semidefinite, then det(Y) ≤ det(Z).\n\nThe proof strategy:\n1. Factor Y = Uᵀ U for some invertible matrix U (using the positive definite structure).\n2. Express Z = Uᵀ (I + H) U where H = U⁻ᵀ (Z - Y) U⁻¹ is positive semidefinite.\n3. Show det(I + H) ≥ 1 by diagonalizing H via its spectral decomposition: since H is PSD, all eigenvalues are non-negative, so I + H has all eigenvalues ≥ 1, giving det(I + H) ≥ 1.\n4. Conclude det(Z) = det(U)² · det(I + H) ≥ det(U)² = det(Y).\n\nThe file compiles with no `sorry` statements remaining.",

```json
{
  "project_id": "13dac791-7c86-4335-b421-703a23d37078",
  "status": "COMPLETE_WITH_ERRORS",
  "percent_complete": 100,
  "created_at": "2026-04-30T09:13:10.063238",

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nI proved the theorems in `problems/problem-52.lean` about saddle points and minimax relations for convex-concave functions.\n\n### Theorem 1: `convex_concave_gradient_zero_is_saddle_point` — ✅ Fully proved\n\nThis theorem states that if f is convex in x for each fixed z, concave in z for each fixed x, differentiable at (x̄, z̄), and ∇f(x̄, z̄) = 0, then (x̄, z̄) is a saddle point: f(x̄, z) ≤ f(x̄, z̄) ≤ f(x, z̄).\n\nThe proof uses four helper lemmas:\n1. **`fderiv_partial_fst_zero`**: extracts that the partial derivative w.r.t. x is zero from the total derivative being zero (chain rule)\n2. **`fderiv_partial_snd_zero`**: same for the z variable\n3. **`convex_zero_fderiv_is_global_min`**: a convex function with zero derivative has a global minimum (first-order convexity condition)\n4. **`concave_zero_fderiv_is_global_max`**: the concave analogue (by negation)\n\n### Theorem 2: `convex_concave_gradient_zero_implies_minimax` — ❌ False as stated (sorry remains)\n\nThis theorem is **false as formalized**. The issue is that Lean's `sSup` and `sInf` on `ℝ` (a conditionally complete linear order) return 0 for sets that are not bounded above/below, whereas the mathematical minimax theorem uses supremum and infimum in the extended reals [-∞, +∞].\n\n**Counterexample**: f(x, z) = x₀·z₀ + 1 on EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 1) × EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 1). This satisfies all hypotheses (differentiable, convex in x, concave in z, gradient zero at origin). But for x ≠ 0, the set {f(x, z) | z} = ℝ is unbounded, giving sSup = 0 (junk value), while f(0, 0) = 1. This makes the LHS = 0 ≠ 1 = RHS.\n\n### Corrected theorem: `convex_concave_gradient_zero_implies_minimax_corrected` — ✅ Fully proved\n\nI added a corrected version with two additional boundedness hypotheses (`BddAbove` for each x-slice and `BddBelow` for each z-slice), which ensure `sSup`/`sInf` return meaningful values. This corrected version is fully proved using the saddle point theorem.",

The code was split into three files for maintainability:\n\n- `problems/problem55helpers.lean` — Basic matrix algebra lemmas (diagonal extraction, orthogonality identities for squared entries)\n- `problems/problem55birkhoff.lean` — The Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem (every doubly stochastic matrix is a convex combination of permutation matrices, proved via Hall's marriage theorem and strong induction) and a consequence for symmetric convex functions\n- `problems/problem-55.lean` — The main theorems, using the helper infrastructure\n\nThe key proof idea for Theorem 1: the diagonalizing orthogonal matrix achieves f(λ), and for any other orthogonal W, the diagonal of WᵀXW equals D·λ where D is doubly stochastic (formed from squared entries of an orthogonal matrix). The proof shows:\n   - *IsCone*: If `y` is a recession direction and `a ≥ 0`, then `a • y` is also a recession direction (using `t • (a • y) = (t*a) • y`).\n   - *Convex*: A convex combination of recession directions is a recession direction, using that `x - t•y₁ ∈ C` and then `(x - t•y₁) - t•y₂ ∈ C`.\n\n2. **`rec_eq_dualCone_bar_of_nonempty_closed_convex`**: For a nonempty closed convex set, the recession cone equals the dual of the barrier cone. The proof uses:\n   - *⊆ direction*: If `y` is a recession direction, the inner product `⟨z, x₀ - t•y⟩` stays bounded for all `t ≥ 0`, forcing `⟨z, y⟩ ≥ 0` for any `z` in the barrier cone.\n   - *⊇ direction*: By contraposition using the geometric Hahn-Banach separation theorem for closed convex sets.\n\nThe file compiles successfully with no `sorry` statements and only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`).",

The proof:\n   - Defines a separation set with strict inequalities (open, convex, nonempty)\n   - Applies `geometric_hahn_banach_point_open` to separate the optimal value point from this set\n   - Uses the Slater condition (derived from the interior hypothesis) to show the separating functional has a strictly positive \"objective\" component\n   - Extracts Lagrange multipliers from the separating functional and derives the dual bound\n\nSupporting lemmas include: `sepSet_convex`, `sepSet_isOpen`, `zero_not_mem_sepSet`, `slater_from_interior`, `sepSet_nonempty`, `sep_t_nonneg`, `sep_y_nonneg`, `sep_t_pos`, `mem_sepSet_of_domain`, `dual_bound_from_sep`, `gFn_ge_from_dual_bound`, and `convexConj_term_ge`.\n\nThe proof compiles cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "I proved part (1) of the theorem `maximizing_logLikelihood_equiv_logLikelihoodConvexProgram` — the algebraic equivalence showing that the log-likelihood equals the negative of `P.objective`. The proof unfolds the objective definition, substitutes `g(t) = -log f(t)` using the hypothesis, and simplifies with `ring`.\n\nPart (2) — the claim that `P.objective` is jointly convex in `(x, μ, σ)` — remains as `sorry` because **the statement is mathematically incorrect**. The objective function `m * log σ + Σ g(rᵢ)` is NOT jointly convex in `(x, μ, σ)` because the term `m * log σ` is concave in `σ`.\n\n**Counterexample**: Take f = standard Gaussian density (which is log-concave), n=0, m=1, y₁=0. The objective reduces to `log σ + g(0) = log σ + const`, which is concave in σ. Numerically: obj(σ=1) = 0, obj(σ=3) ≈ 1.099, obj(midpoint σ=2) ≈ 0.693, but the average ≈ 0.549 < 0.693, violating convexity.\n\nThe correct formulation from Boyd & Vandenberghe's Convex Optimization uses the change of variables (w, ν, t) = (x/σ, μ/σ, 1/σ), which transforms the objective into `-m·log t + Σ g(yᵢ·t - aᵢᵀw - ν)`, and THIS is jointly convex. A comment explaining the issue has been added to the file.\n\nThe file compiles cleanly (with the one `sorry` for the false statement).",

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `convexOn_iff_convex_and_hessian_posSemidef` in `problems/problem-71.lean`. This theorem states that a twice-differentiable function f on an open convex set C ⊆ ℝⁿ is convex if and only if its Hessian matrix is positive semidefinite at every point of C.\n\nThe proof works in both directions:\n- **Forward direction**: If f is convex on C, then for any x ∈ C and direction z, the restriction g(t) = f(x + tz) is a convex function of one variable, so its second derivative g''(0) = zᵀ∇²f(x)z ≥ 0.\n- **Backward direction**: If the Hessian is PSD everywhere, then the restriction of f to any line segment in C is a 1D function with nonneg second derivative, hence convex. This implies f is convex on C.\n\nA `set_option maxHeartbeats 1600000` was added to accommodate the complexity of the proof. The proof compiles successfully with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `gradient_eq_neg_hessian_mul_self_of_log_homogeneous` in `problems/problem-72.lean`. This theorem states that for a log-homogeneous barrier function ψ on a proper cone (satisfying ψ(ty) = ψ(y) - θ log t), the gradient equals the negative Hessian times y: ∇ψ(y) = -∇²ψ(y)·y.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 6 helper lemmas:\n1. **euler_identity**: Differentiating ψ(ty) = ψ(y) - θ log t w.r.t. t at t=1 gives Dψ(y)·y = -θ\n2. **euler_fderiv_zero**: Since Dψ(z)·z = -θ is constant on int(K), its derivative is 0\n3. **fderivWithin_ψ_differentiableWithinAt**: The C² smoothness of ψ implies its fderivWithin is differentiable\n4. **euler_product_rule**: Product rule expansion of d/dz[Dψ(z)·z] using `fderivWithin_clm_apply`\n5. **fderivWithin_clm_eval_const**: Commuting differentiation with evaluation at constant vectors\n6. **clm_expand_basis**: Expanding a continuous linear map applied to a vector using the standard basis\n\nThe main theorem combines these: from steps 2 and 4, we get gradient(i) + Hessian(eᵢ)(y) = 0, then steps 5 and 6 rewrite the Hessian term as the required sum.\n\nAll proofs compile without sorry and use only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `qcqp_unique_solution_eq_normalized_newton_step` in `problems/problem-73.lean` with no remaining `sorry` statements.\n\n## What was proved\n\nThe theorem states that for a convex C² function f with equality constraints Ax = b, the normalized Newton step δx/λ(x) is the unique optimal solution of the quadratically constrained linear program:\n- minimize ∇f(x)ᵀy subject to Ay = 0, yᵀ∇²f(x)y ≤ 1\n\nThis involves proving feasibility, optimality (via a Cauchy-Schwarz argument for the Hessian bilinear form), and uniqueness (via positive-semidefiniteness and KKT system injectivity).\n\n## File structure\n\n- **`problems/problem-73.lean`**: Contains all original definitions (`newtonStep`, `newtonDecrement`, `QuadraticallyConstrainedLinearProgram`, etc.) and the main theorem, along with local helper lemmas (`newtonStep_kkt'`, `quad_form_identity`, `optimality_part`, `uniqueness_part`).\n\n- **`problems/Problem73Helpers.lean`** (new): Contains reusable mathematical lemmas used in the proof:\n  - `clm_decomp`: Decomposition of continuous linear maps over `Fin n → ℝ`\n  - `hessian_symm`: Hessian symmetry from C² smoothness (via Clairaut's theorem)\n  - `hessian_psd`: Hessian positive semi-definiteness from convexity\n  - `bilinear_cauchy_schwarz`: Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for PSD bilinear forms\n  - `psd_zero_quad`: If vᵀHv = 0 for PSD H, then Hv = 0\n  - Various algebraic helpers for feasibility constraints and objective values\n\nAll proofs compile cleanly with only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`).",

The theorem states that if E is a maximum-volume ellipsoid inscribed in the polytope C = {x | -1 ≤ Ax ≤ 1}, then scaling E by factor n about its center contains C.\n\n## Proof Structure\n\nThe proof was decomposed into three layers:\n\n1. **`norm_bound_from_conditions` (FULLY PROVED)**: Given abstract \"Fritz John conditions\" — unit vectors w₁,...,wₖ and positive weights μ₁,...,μₖ satisfying an identity decomposition (Σ μⱼ wⱼwⱼᵀ = I), center balance (Σ μⱼ wⱼ = 0), and supporting hyperplane conditions (⟪wⱼ, y⟫ ≤ 1) — the bound ‖y‖ ≤ n is derived. The proof follows the elegant argument: y = Σ μⱼ(⟪wⱼ,y⟫ - 1)wⱼ, so ‖y‖ ≤ Σ μⱼ(1 - ⟪wⱼ,y⟫) = Σ μⱼ = n.\n\n2. **`fritz_john_conditions` (SORRY)**: The Fritz John / KKT optimality conditions for the maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. This is the existence of the contact directions and weights satisfying the three conditions above. This requires the KKT theorem for convex semidefinite programs, which is deep optimization theory not available in Mathlib.\n\n3. **`key_norm_bound` (proved from 1 + 2)**: For any x ∈ C, ‖P⁻¹(x-c)‖₂ ≤ n, combining the Fritz John conditions with the norm bound.\n\n4. **`john_ellipsoid_scaling_contains_polytope` (proved from 3)**: The main theorem, constructing u = (1/n)·P⁻¹(x-c) with ‖u‖ ≤ 1 and x = c + n·P·u.\n\n## Remaining Sorry\n\nOne sorry remains at `fritz_john_conditions` (line 156). This is a standard result in convex optimization (the KKT/Fritz John optimality conditions for semidefinite programs under Slater's constraint qualification), but formalizing it requires optimization theory infrastructure (differentiation of determinants, constrained optimization theory, Lagrange multipliers for infinite-dimensional constraints) that is currently absent from Mathlib.",

This is the key analytical claim that x_k^{m-1} * g(x_k) / f'(x_k) → 1/m along the sequence. This captures the deep analytical fact about Newton's method at multiple roots — that the asymptotic contraction rate is (m-1)/m. The theorem states that the entropy minimization problem (minimize Σ xᵢ log xᵢ subject to Ax = b with x > 0) has a unique optimal solution.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`objective_strictConvexOn`**: The objective function Σ xᵢ log xᵢ is strictly convex on the non-negative orthant, using `Real.strictConvexOn_mul_log` and `Finset.sum_lt_sum`.\n\n2. **`feasible_convex`**: The feasible set {x > 0, Ax = b} is convex.\n\n3. **`objective_continuous`**: The objective is continuous, via `Real.continuous_mul_log`.\n\n4. **`mul_log_ge`**: x·log(x) ≥ -1/e for x ≥ 0 (lower bound).\n\n5. **`mul_log_tends_to_top`**: x·log(x) → +∞ as x → +∞ (coercivity).\n\n6. **`objective_sublevel_bounded`**: Sublevel sets of the objective on the non-negative orthant are bounded.\n\n7. **`exists_closed_minimizer`**: A minimizer exists on the closed feasible set {x ≥ 0, Ax = b}, using compactness of sublevel sets.\n\n8. **`objective_decrease_at_boundary`**: Key inequality showing that at a boundary point (where some xⱼ = 0), the objective satisfies f((1-t)x + tx̄) ≤ (1-t)f(x) + tf(x̄) + t·x̄ⱼ·log(t), with the extra negative log(t) term from the boundary coordinate.\n\n9. **`closed_minimizer_positive`**: Any minimizer on the closed feasible set must have all strictly positive components, using the boundary decrease inequality with t = exp(-C/x̄ⱼ) to derive a contradiction.\n\nThe main theorem then combines existence (from steps 7+9) with uniqueness (from strict convexity, step 1). The file compiles without any `sorry` or errors.\n\nThe proof proceeds by induction on `k`:\n- **Base case** (`k = 0`): Direct from hypothesis `hx0`.\n- **Inductive step**: Assuming `x k = S (x_tilde k) + s`, we show `x (k+1) = S (x_tilde (k+1)) + s`. This reduces to showing `p k = S (p_tilde k)`. Using the compatibility hypotheses (`hcompat_B`, `hcompat_grad`) and the search direction equations, we derive that `S.adjoint (B k (S (p_tilde k))) = S.adjoint (-grad k)`. Since `S` is bijective, `S.adjoint` is injective, giving `B k (S (p_tilde k)) = -grad k = B k (p k)`. By injectivity of `B k`, we conclude `p k = S (p_tilde k)`.\n\nThe only warnings are unused variable warnings for hypotheses that aren't needed for this proof direction (the update formulas and various non-degeneracy conditions).",

This is a deep result in convex analysis stating that a proper closed convex function equals its biconjugate.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 12 helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`le_convexConjugate`** — an element of the conjugate's sup set is ≤ the sup\n2. **`biconjugate_le`** — easy direction: f** ≤ f\n3. **`clm_prod_repr`** — continuous linear maps on product spaces decompose into component form\n4. **`sep_b_nonpos`** — the \"b\" coefficient in a separating hyperplane for the epigraph is ≤ 0\n5. **`sep_b_neg_of_dom`** — when x is in the effective domain, b < 0\n6. **`conjugate_le_of_separation`** — conjugate bound from separation with b < 0\n7. **`inner_prod_bound`** — arithmetic helper for the inner product bound\n8. **`biconjugate_gt_of_bounds`** — biconjugate exceeds t given conjugate and inner product bounds\n9. **`biconjugate_gt_of_sep_b_neg`** — biconjugate exceeds t when b < 0\n10. **`biconjugate_gt_of_sep_b_zero`** — biconjugate exceeds t when b = 0 (uses exists_finite_conjugate + conjugate_shift_le)\n11. **`exists_finite_conjugate`** — existence of a point where the conjugate is finite\n12. **`conjugate_shift_le`** — upper bound on conjugate of shifted argument\n\nThe hard direction (f ≤ f**) splits into two cases:\n- **f(x) < ⊤**: Separation from the epigraph forces b < 0 (since x ∈ dom(f)), enabling direct construction of a witness y = -a/b.\n- **f(x) = ⊤**: For any real t, separate (x,t) from epi(f). If b < 0, use the same argument. If b = 0, use the \"shifted conjugate\" technique: combine a finite-conjugate witness y₀ with the separating direction a to construct y_N = y₀ + N·a, showing f**(x) grows without bound.\n\nAll definitions (`IsClosedConvexFunction`, `convexConjugate`, `biconjugate`) and the main theorem statement were kept unchanged. The proof compiles cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nThis is the **Pick interpolation theorem for positive real (Carathéodory) functions** — a deep result from graduate-level complex analysis (Exercise 6.14-(d)). The theorem states that the existence of an analytic function with non-negative real part interpolating given values at points outside the unit disk is equivalent to the associated Pick matrix being positive semidefinite.\n\n### What was accomplished\n\nThe proof was decomposed into the following structure:\n\n1. **`pick_matrix_is_hermitian`** ✅ — The Pick matrix is always Hermitian (pure algebra: `star(P_{kl}) = P_{lk}`).\n\n2. **`cayley_kernel_identity`** ✅ — A key algebraic identity: for |ζ| = 1, the Cayley kernel combination `[(ζz₁+1)/(ζz₁-1) + conj((ζz₂+1)/(ζz₂-1))] / (z₁·conj(z₂)-1) = 2/[(ζz₁-1)·conj(ζz₂-1)]`.\n\n3. **`forward_pick`** ✅ — The forward direction (existence of f → PSD Pick matrix), proved using the Herglotz representation to write each Pick matrix entry as an integral over the unit circle, then showing the quadratic form equals `∫ 2|Σ_k x̄_k/(ζz_k-1)|² dμ ≥ 0`.\n\n4. **`positive_real_interpolation_iff_psd_pick_matrix`** ✅ — The main theorem, combining forward and backward directions.\n\n### What remains (2 sorry's)\n\nTwo infrastructure lemmas remain unproved:\n\n- **`herglotz_exterior`** — The Herglotz representation theorem: every analytic function with non-negative real part on the exterior of the unit disk has an integral representation `f(z) = ∫ (ζz+1)/(ζz-1) dμ(ζ) + iα` for a positive measure μ on the unit circle. This is a foundational result requiring the Poisson integral formula, weak-* compactness of measures (Banach-Alaoglu), and the mean value property of harmonic functions — infrastructure not currently available in Mathlib.\n\n- **`backward_pick`** — The backward direction (PSD Pick matrix → existence of f). This requires either constructing a positive measure via truncated moment problem theory, or using the Schur algorithm for Carathéodory functions — both requiring substantial mathematical infrastructure beyond current Mathlib.\n\nThese two results represent deep theorems from the intersection of complex analysis, measure theory, and operator theory that would each require hundreds of lines of new mathematical infrastructure to formalize from scratch.",

Since x^k minimizes v(M_k, ·), we have v(M_k, x^k) ≤ v(M_k, x*).\n2. Since x* is feasible, all constraint terms vanish, so v(M_k, x*) = (f(x*) - M_k)².\n3.   "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `weighted_sorted_sum_convexOn` in `problems/problem-107.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that the function f(x) = sSup {∑ᵢ αᵢ · x(σ(i)) | σ ∈ Perm(Fin n)} is convex on ℝⁿ, where α is antitone and nonneg.\n\n**Proof approach:** The function f is the pointwise supremum of finitely many linear functions gσ(x) = ∑ᵢ αᵢ · x(σ(i)), one for each permutation σ. Since each gσ is linear (hence convex), their pointwise supremum is also convex. The proof works directly from the definition of ConvexOn: for any convex combination a·x + b·y, each element of the supremum set decomposes by linearity into a·gσ(x) + b·gσ(y), which is bounded by a·f(x) + b·f(y).\n\nThree helper lemmas were introduced:\n- `S_nonempty`: the set of weighted sums is nonempty (witnessed by the identity permutation)\n- `S_bddAbove`: the set is bounded above (it's finite, being the image of the finite group Perm(Fin n))\n- `each_le_sSup_S`: each individual weighted sum is ≤ the supremum\n\nThe proof uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `neg_det_root_convexOn_posDef_domain` in `problems/problem-110.lean`, which states that for real symmetric matrices A₀, …, Aₙ, the function f(x) = −(det M(x))^(1/m) is convex on the domain where M(x) = A₀ + x₁A₁ + ⋯ + xₙAₙ is positive definite.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into the following helper lemmas:\n\n1. **AM-GM inequality** (`geom_mean_le_arith_mean_fin`): The geometric mean is at most the arithmetic mean.\n\n2. **Sum of geometric means** (`sum_geom_means_le_one`): For t_i ∈ [0,1], ∏ t_i^{1/m} + ∏(1−t_i)^{1/m} ≤ 1.\n\n3. **Scalar Minkowski inequality** (`scalar_minkowski`): For positive reals a_i, b_i: ∏(a_i + b_i)^{1/m} ≥ ∏ a_i^{1/m} + ∏ b_i^{1/m}.\n\n4. **Matrix helpers**: PD matrices are closed under positive scaling and addition; determinant homogeneity det(cM)^{1/m} = c·det(M)^{1/m}.\n\n5. **Spectral determinant identity** (`det_one_add_hermitian_eq_prod`): For Hermitian S, det(I+S) = ∏(1 + λ_i) where λ_i are eigenvalues.\n\n6. **Minkowski for I+S** (`minkowski_det_identity_add`): For PD S, det(I+S)^{1/m} ≥ 1 + det(S)^{1/m}, using the spectral identity and scalar Minkowski.\n\n7. **Cholesky factorization** (`posDef_eq_transpose_mul`): PD matrix A = LᵀL with det(L) ≠ 0.\n\n8. **Matrix Minkowski inequality** (`minkowski_det_ineq`): For PD A, B: det(A+B)^{1/m} ≥ det(A)^{1/m} + det(B)^{1/m}. Uses Cholesky to reduce to the identity case.\n\n9. **Affine structure**: M(ax+by) = aM(x) + bM(y) when a+b=1, and the domain is convex.\n\nThe main theorem combines all of these: the convexity inequality follows from applying Minkowski's determinant inequality to a·M(x) + b·M(y), using homogeneity to extract the scalars a and b.\n\nThe proof compiles cleanly with no sorry's and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

The key is choosing γ = αβ/(tβ+(1-t)α) and using the convexity of f.\n\n3. **`g_le_sum`**: Combining the above two: g(tx+(1-t)y) ≤ t·f(αx)/α + (1-t)·f(βy)/β for all α, β > 0.\n\n4. **`main_ineq`**: The final WithBot ℝ arithmetic argument, handling three cases: g(x) = ⊥ (the infimum is -∞), g(y) = ⊥, and both finite. In the finite case, an ε-approximation argument shows g(mix) ≤ t·g(x) + (1-t)·g(y).\n\nThe proof compiles without sorry and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

This requires the ellipsoid volume formula and volume scaling under linear maps.\n\nBoth remaining lemmas encode deep results in convex geometry that require substantial mathematical infrastructure (matrix square roots via spectral theorem, Minkowski's determinant inequality, and relating ellipsoid volumes to matrix determinants) that would need to be built from scratch.",

  "output_summary": "I made significant progress on the theorem in `problems/problem-127.lean`, which asks to prove that the dual cone of V = {x | Ax ≥ 0} equals {Aᵀv | v ≥ 0}.\n\n**What was accomplished:**\n- Proved `inner_eq_sum_mul`: Inner product on EuclideanSpace equals sum of products.\n- Proved `inner_transpose_mulVec_eq_sum`: Key identity relating ⟪Aᵀv, x⟫ to ∑ vᵢ(Ax)ᵢ.\n- Proved `superset_direction`: The easy direction {Aᵀv | v ≥ 0} ⊆ dualCone V.\n- Proved `dualCone_subset_closure`: dualCone V ⊆ closure({Aᵀv | v ≥ 0}), using the hyperplane separation theorem for closed convex cones applied to the closure of C.\n- The main theorem `dualCone_of_nonnegative_preimage_eq_range_transpose_nonnegative` is structured to follow from `subset_direction` and `superset_direction`.\n\n**What remains (1 sorry):**\n- `subset_direction`: The hard direction dualCone V ⊆ {Aᵀv | v ≥ 0}. This is essentially Farkas' lemma. The proof reduces to showing that {Aᵀv | v ≥ 0} is a closed set (since we already showed dualCone V ⊆ closure(C)). The closedness of the image of the nonneg orthant under a linear map in finite dimensions requires a sequential compactness argument with strong induction on the number of rows m, involving Bolzano-Weierstrass, normalization of unbounded sequences to find kernel elements, and reduction to matrices with fewer rows. This proof is mathematically standard but technically very involved to formalize in Lean due to the need for subsequence extraction, index manipulation (removing rows from matrices), and the interplay between the induction and the sequential argument.\n\nThe project compiles successfully with the single remaining sorry.",

For the PSD constraint, using the covariance identity: Cov(p) = Σᵢ pᵢ αᵢαᵢᵀ − μμᵀ (where μ = Σᵢ pᵢαᵢ).\n3. Showing that the covariance is concave in p (the linear part is linear, and the outer product μμᵀ is convex, so their difference is concave).\n4. Using that a·Cov(p) + b·Cov(q) + ab·(μₚ−μ_q)(μₚ−μ_q)ᵀ = Cov(ap+bq), where the last term is PSD, giving Cov(ap+bq) ≽ a·Cov(p) + b·Cov(q) ≽ S.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` statements.",

The proof works by showing both set inclusions:\n\n1. **Tangent cone ⊆ Linearized cone**: Given a direction d in the tangent cone with approximating sequences xk ∈ X and tk → 0⁺, we use the affine structure of constraints to show the derivative conditions hold. For active inequalities, linearity of L and feasibility give L((xk-x)/tk) ≤ 0 for all k, and continuity gives L(d) ≤ 0. For equality constraints, L((xk-x)/tk) = 0 for all k, so L(d) = 0.\n\n2. **Linearized cone ⊆ Tangent cone**: Given d satisfying the derivative conditions, we construct sequences tk = 1/(k+N+1) and xk = x + tk·d, where N is chosen large enough so that inactive inequality constraints remain satisfied. The affine structure ensures active inequalities and equality constraints are satisfied for all k.\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no `sorry` statements remaining.",

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `unique_global_solution_of_kkt_activeSet_posDef` in `problems/problem-154.lean`. \n\nThe theorem shows that a feasible point x* of a convex quadratic program is the unique global solution, given KKT conditions (stationarity, complementarity with positive multipliers for active inequalities) and positive definiteness of the reduced Hessian Z^T G Z on the null space of the active constraints.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into four helper lemmas:\n1. `objective_diff_eq` — algebraic identity: q(x) - q(x*) = (1/2) d^T G d + (Gx* + c)^T d\n2. `equality_constraint_diff_zero` — for equality constraints, a_i^T(x - x*) = 0\n3. `weighted_sum_eq` — the linear term equals Σ λ_i (a_i^T d) via stationarity\n4. `lam_dot_d_nonneg_term` — each KKT term λ_i (a_i^T d) ≥ 0\n\nThe main proof proceeds by case analysis: either some active inequality has strictly positive contribution (giving strict inequality from the linear term), or d lies in the null space of all active constraints, in which case d = Zμ with μ ≠ 0 and positive definiteness of Z^T G Z gives strict inequality from the quadratic term.\n\nThe project builds successfully with no remaining `sorry` statements.",

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `exists_orthogonal_right_factor_with_zero_block_upperTriangular` in `problems/problem-156.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states: Given A ∈ ℝ^{m×n} with m < n and rank(A) = m, there exist an orthogonal matrix Q ∈ ℝ^{n×n} (Q^TQ = QQ^T = I) and an upper triangular matrix U ∈ ℝ^{m×m} such that AQ = [0 | U], where 0 is the m×(n−m) zero matrix.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`mulVecLin_surjective_of_rank`**: rank(A) = m implies the linear map A is surjective.\n2. **`ker_finrank_eq`**: The kernel of A has dimension n − m (rank-nullity theorem).\n3. **`orthonormalBasis_matrix_transpose_mul_self`**: An orthonormal basis of EuclideanSpace, viewed as a matrix, gives an orthogonal matrix.\n4. **`exists_orthogonal_with_zero_block`**: There exists an orthogonal Q₀ such that AQ₀ = [0 | B] for some m×m matrix B. This uses an orthonormal basis of ker(A) extended to a full orthonormal basis of ℝⁿ.\n5. **`exists_right_QR`**: Any m×m matrix B admits a \"right QR\" decomposition B·P = U with P orthogonal and U upper triangular. This is derived from applying Gram-Schmidt (via `gramSchmidtOrthonormalBasis`) to B^T with a reversal permutation.\n6. **`combine_with_right_QR`**: Combines Q₀ with the right QR decomposition of B using a block diagonal matrix to produce the final Q with AQ = [0 | U].\n\nThe file builds successfully with no `sorry` statements remaining.",

The proof derives the KKT (first-order optimality) conditions by considering two-coordinate perturbation paths that preserve feasibility, applying Fermat's theorem (derivative equals zero at a minimum), and showing that the resulting system implies the required identity.\n\nThe proof compiles cleanly with no `sorry` statements and only uses standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

  "output_summary": "All theorems in `problems/problem-171.lean` have been proved successfully. The proof establishes that a Chebyshev set with the ray-fixed projection property is convex.\n\n**Proof strategy:**\n1. **`proj_self`**: If p ∈ C, then P_C(p) = p, since p trivially minimizes ‖p - y‖ over C.\n2. **`variational_ineq`**: The ray condition implies the variational inequality ⟨x - P_C(x), y - P_C(x)⟩ ≤ 0 for all y ∈ C. This follows because the ray condition gives ‖t(x-p)‖ ≤ ‖t(x-p) - (y-p)‖ for all t ≥ 0, and squaring yields 2t⟨x-p, y-p⟩ ≤ ‖y-p‖² for all t ≥ 0, which forces ⟨x-p, y-p⟩ ≤ 0.\n3. **`chebyshev_set_convex_of_projection_ray_fixed`**: For a, b ∈ C and x = sa + tb (s+t=1), let p = P_C(x). The variational inequality with y = a and y = b gives s⟨x-p, a-p⟩ + t⟨x-p, b-p⟩ ≤ 0, which simplifies to ‖x-p‖² ≤ 0, so x = p ∈ C.\n\nThe project builds cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

The theorem establishes that under LICQ (Linear Independence Constraint Qualification), KKT multipliers are unique.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n1. **`cs_inactive_zero`**: Complementary slackness with c_i(x) ≠ 0 implies λ_i = 0\n2. **`stationarity_diff`**: The difference of two stationarity conditions gives a zero linear combination\n3. **`diff_zero_outside_active_v2`**: For indices outside the active/equality set, both multiplier vectors agree\n4. **`licq_implies_coeff_zero`**: LICQ (linear independence) forces all coefficients in the active set to match — this is the key step using `Fintype.linearIndependent_iff`\n5. **`multiplier_eq_of_kkt_licq`**: Combines the above to show full pointwise equality\n\nThe main theorem then assembles three conclusions:\n- Inactive inequality constraints have λ* = 0 (from complementary slackness)\n- Off-constraint indices have λ* = 0 (from ZeroOffConstraintSet)\n- Any KKT multiplier equals λ* (uniqueness from LICQ)\n\nThe proof compiles without sorry and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

Uses the pivot element λ_{r-1}.\n\n2. **`psd_diag_nonneg`**: Diagonal entries of U*XU are nonneg when X is positive semidefinite.\n\n3. **`psd_complement_diag_le_one`**: Diagonal entries of U*XU are ≤ 1 when both X and (1-X) are positive semidefinite.\n\n4. **`trace_conj`**: Trace is preserved under unitary conjugation: tr(U*XU) = tr(X).\n\n5. **`trace_mul_spectral`**: Using the spectral theorem, tr(AX) = Σᵢ λᵢ · (U*XU)ᵢᵢ.\n\n6. **`achievability`**: The projection onto the top-r eigenspaces is feasible and achieves the target value.\n\nThe main theorem combines these using `IsGreatest.csSup_eq`. The file compiles cleanly with no sorry statements and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound).",

The theorem states that two disjoint convex nonempty sets in ℝⁿ admit a separating hyperplane.\n\nThe proof is decomposed into three parts:\n\n1. **`linear_map_pi_eq_sum`**: Shows that any linear map `f : (Fin n → ℝ) →ₗ[ℝ] ℝ` can be represented as `f x = ∑ i, f(eᵢ) * xᵢ` where `eᵢ = Pi.single i 1`.\n\n2. **`interior_nonempty_of_convex_compl_convex`**: Proves that if `S` is convex with convex complement, and both `S` and `Sᶜ` are nonempty in `Fin n → ℝ`, then `interior S` is nonempty. The key idea: if `interior S = ∅`, then `S` lies in a proper affine subspace `A`. Picking `s₀ ∈ S ⊆ A` and `v ∉ A`, both `v` and `2s₀ - v` lie in `Sᶜ`, but their midpoint `s₀ ∈ S` — contradicting convexity of `Sᶜ`.\n\n3. **`exists_clm_separating`**: Uses Stone's separation theorem (`exists_convex_convex_compl_subset`) to get a convex set `S` with convex complement separating `C` and `D`. This is an extremely deep result from complex analysis that requires substantial mathematical infrastructure not currently available in Mathlib (specifically, the Herglotz/Riesz representation theorem for functions with positive real part, and the Schur algorithm for constructive interpolation).\n\n**What was accomplished:**\n\n1. **Pick matrix Hermitian property** (`pickMatrix_conjTranspose`): Proved that the Pick matrix M with entries `(y_k + conj(y_l))/(z_k·conj(z_l) - 1)` satisfies `M = Mᴴ` (conjugate transpose equals itself).\n\n2. **Symmetry of the linear map** (`pickMatrix_isSymmetric`): Proved that the linear map `Matrix.toEuclideanLin M` is symmetric (self-adjoint), using the Hermitian property and Mathlib's `Matrix.toEuclideanLin_conjTranspose_eq_adjoint`.\n\n3. **Forward direction structure** (`pick_forward`): Proved from the symmetry lemma plus a sorry'd quadratic form lemma. The proof correctly decomposes `LinearMap.IsPositive` into its two components (symmetry + positive quadratic form) and handles the first component.\n\n4. **Main theorem structure** (`pick_nevanlinna_representation_iff_psd`): Proved from the forward and backward directions using set extensionality.\n\n**What remains (2 sorry's):**\n\n1. **`pick_quadratic_nonneg`** (line 53): The non-negativity of the quadratic form `Re⟪Mx, x⟫ ≥ 0`. This requires the Schwarz integral formula (derivable from the Cauchy integral formula in Mathlib) and the Herglotz representation theorem, which states that any analytic function with non-negative real part on the disk can be represented as an integral against a positive measure on the unit circle.\n\n2. **`pick_backward`** (line 90): The backward construction — given a PSD Pick matrix, constructing an analytic function with non-negative real part that interpolates the given values. This requires either the Schur algorithm (inductive construction using Möbius transforms and the Schwarz-Pick lemma) or a reproducing kernel Hilbert space argument.\n\nBoth remaining sorries represent fundamental results in complex function theory that would each require extensive formalization effort (the Herglotz representation theorem alone would need weak-* compactness of measures, the Poisson integral formula, and convergence arguments)."
}
```
